| EDWARD | Even as thou wilt, sweet Warwick, let it be.<br>For in thy shoulder do I build my seat,<br>And never will I undertake the thing<br>Wherein thy counsel and consent is wanting.<br>[[Richard]], I will create thee [[Duke of Gloucester]],<br>And George, of [[Clarence]]; Warwick, as ourself,<br>Shall do and undo as him pleaseth best. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Let me be Duke of [[Clarence]], George of [[Gloucester]]—<br>For [[Gloucester]]’s dukedom is too ominous. |
| WARWICK | Tut, that’s a foolish observation—<br>[[Richard]], be [[Duke of Gloucester]]. Now to [[London]]<br>To see these honours in possession. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt. York’s head is removed |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter two Gamekeepers, with crossbows in their hands |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | Under this thick-grown brake we’ll shroud ourselves,<br>For through this laund anon the deer will come,<br>And in this covert will we make our stand,<br>Culling the principal of all the deer. |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | I’ll stay above the [[Hill\|hill]], so both may shoot. |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | That cannot be—the noise of thy crossbow<br>Will scare the herd, and so my shoot is lost.<br>Here stand we both, and aim we at the best.<br>And, for the [[Time\|time]] shall not seem tedious,<br>I’ll tell thee what befell me on a day<br>In this [[Self\|self]] place where now we mean to stand. |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | Here comes a man—let’s stay till he be past.<br>They stand apart. Enter [[King]] Henry, disguised, carrying a [[Prayer\|prayer]]-book |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | From Scotland am I stolen, even of pure [[Love\|love]],<br>To greet mine own land with my wishful sight.<br>No, Harry, Harry—’tis no land of thine.<br>Thy place is filled, thy sceptre wrung from thee,<br>Thy balm washed off wherewith thou wast anointed.<br>No bending knee will call thee Caesar now,<br>No humble suitors press to speak for right,<br>No, not a man comes for redress of thee—<br>For how can I help them and not myself? |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | (to the Second Gamekeeper)<br>Ay, here’s a deer whose skin’s a keeper’s fee:<br>This is the quondam [[King\|king]]—let’s seize upon him. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Let me embrace thee, sour adversity,<br>For wise men say it is the wisest course. |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | (to the First Gamekeeper)<br>Why linger we? Let us lay hands upon him. |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | (to the Second Gamekeeper)<br>Forbear awhile—we’ll hear a little more. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | My [[Queen\|queen]] and son are gone to France for aid,<br>And, as I hear, the great commanding Warwick<br>Is thither gone to crave the French [[King]]’s sister<br>To wife for Edward. If this news be true,<br>Poor [[Queen]] and son, your labour is but lost—<br>For Warwick is a subtle orator,<br>And Louis a prince soon won with moving words.<br>By this account, then, Margaret may win him—<br>For she’s a woman to be pitied much.<br>Her sighs will make a batt‘ry in his breast,<br>Her [[Tears\|tears]] will pierce into a marble heart,<br>The tiger will be mild whiles she doth mourn,<br>And Nero will be tainted with remorse<br>To hear and see her plaints, her brinish [[Tears\|tears]].<br>Ay, but she’s come to beg; Warwick to give.<br>She on his left side, craving aid for Henry;<br>He on his right, asking a wife for Edward.<br>She weeps and says her Henry is deposed,<br>He smiles and says his Edward is installed;<br>That she, poor wretch, for grief can speak no more,<br>Whiles Warwick tells his title, smooths the wrong,<br>Inferreth arguments of mighty strength,<br>And in conclusion wins the [[King]] from her<br>With promise of his sister and what else<br>To strengthen and support [[King]] Edward’s place.<br>O, Margaret, thus ’twill be; and thou, poor [[Soul\|soul]],<br>Art then forsaken, as thou went’st forlorn. |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | (coming orward)<br>Say, what art thou that talk’st of kings and queens? |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | More than I seem, and less than I was born to:<br>A man at least, for less I should not be;<br>And men may talk of kings, and why not I? |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | Ay, but thou talk’st as if thou wert a [[King\|king]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Why, so I am, in mind—and that’s enough. |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | But if thou be a [[King\|king]], where is thy [[Crown\|crown]]? |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | My [[Crown\|crown]] is in my heart, not on my head;<br>Not decked with diamonds and Indian stones,<br>Nor to be seen. My [[Crown\|crown]] is called content—<br>A [[Crown\|crown]] it is that seldom kings enjoy. |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | [[Well]], if you be a [[King\|king]] crowned with content,<br>Your [[Crown\|crown]] content and you must be contented<br>To go along with us—for, as we think,<br>You are the [[King\|king]] [[King]] Edward hath deposed,<br>And we his subjects sworn in all allegiance<br>Will apprehend you as his enemy. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | But did you never swear and break an oath? |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | No—never such an oath, nor will not now. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Where did you dwell when I was [[King]] of England? |
| SECOND GAMEKEEPER | Here in this country, where we now remain. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | I was anointed [[King\|king]] at nine months old,<br>My father and my grandfather were kings,<br>And you were sworn true subjects unto me—<br>And tell me, then, have you not broke your oaths ? |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | No, for we were subjects but while you were [[King\|king]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Why, am I dead? Do I not breathe a man?<br>Ah, simple men, you know not what you swear.<br>Look as I blow this feather from my face,<br>And as the air blows it to me again,<br>Obeying with my [[Wind\|wind]] when I do blow,<br>And yielding to another when it blows,<br>Commanded always by the greater gust—<br>Such is the lightness of you common men.<br>But do not break your oaths, for of that sin<br>My mild entreaty shall not make you guilty.<br>Go where you will, the [[King]] shall be commanded;<br>And be you kings, command, and I’ll obey. |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | We are true subjects to the [[King]], [[King]] Edward. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | So would you be again to Henry,<br>If he were seated as [[King]] Edward is. |
| FIRST GAMEKEEPER | We charge you, in God’s name and in the [[King]]’s,<br>To go with us unto the officers. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | In God’s name, lead; your [[King\|king]]’s name be obeyed;<br>And what God will, that let your [[King\|king]] perform;<br>And what he will I humbly yield unto. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Edward, [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]], George Duke of [[Clarence]], and the Lady Gray |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Brother of [[Gloucester]], at Saint Albans field<br>This lady’s husband, Sir [[Richard]] Gray, was slain,<br>His lands then seized on by the conqueror.<br>Her suit is now to repossess those lands,<br>Which we in justice cannot [[Well\|well]] deny,<br>Because in quarrel of the [[house]] of York<br>The worthy gentleman did lose his life. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Your highness shall do [[Well\|well]] to grant her suit—<br>It were dishonour to deny it her. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | It were no less; but yet I’ll make a pause. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF | [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]], (aside to George) Yea, is it so?<br>I see the lady hath a thing to grant<br>Before the [[King]] will grant her humble suit. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | (aside to [[Richard]])<br>He knows the game; how true he keeps the [[Wind\|wind]]! |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside to George) [[Silence]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Lady Gray)<br>Widow, we will consider of your suit;<br>And come some other [[Time\|time]] to know our mind. |
| LADY GRAY | Right gracious lord, I cannot brook delay.<br>May it please your highness to resolve me now,<br>And what your pleasure is shall satisfy me. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF | [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]], (aside to George)<br>Ay, widow ? Then I’ll warrant you all your lands<br>An if what pleases him shall pleasure you.<br>Fight closer, or, good [[Faith\|faith]], you’ll catch a blow. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | (aside to [[Richard]])<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] her not unless she chance to fall. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside to George)<br>God forbid that! For he’ll take vantages. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Lady Gray)<br>How many children hast thou, widow ? Tell me. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | (aside to [[Richard]])<br>I think he means to beg a [[Child\|child]] of her. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside to George)<br>Nay, whip me then—he’ll rather give her two. |
| LADY GRAY | (to [[King]] Edward) Three, my most gracious lord. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside)<br>You shall have four, an you’ll be ruled by him. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Lady Gray)<br>’Twere pity they should lose their father’s lands. |
| LADY GRAY | Be pitiful, dread lord, and grant it them. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to [[Richard]] and George)<br>Lords, give us leave—I’ll try this widow’s wit. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]], aside to George |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Ay, good leave have you; for you will have leave,<br>Till youth take leave and leave you to the crutch.<br>[[Richard]] and George stand apart |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Lady Gray)<br>Now tell me, madam, do you [[Love\|love]] your children? |
| LADY GRAY | Ay, full as dearly as I [[Love\|love]] myself. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | And would you not do much to do them good? |
| LADY GRAY | To do them good I would sustain some harm. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Then get your husband’s lands, to do them good. |
| LADY GRAY | Therefore I came unto your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | I’ll tell you how these lands are to be got. |
| LADY GRAY | So shall you bind me to your highness’ service. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | What service wilt thou do me, if I give them? |
| LADY GRAY | What you command, that rests in me to do. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | But you will take exceptions to my boon. |
| LADY GRAY | No, gracious lord, except I cannot do it. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Ay, but thou canst do what I mean to ask. |
| LADY GRAY | Why, then, I will do what your grace commands. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to George)<br>He plies her hard, and much rain wears the marble. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | As red as [[Fire\|fire]]! Nay, then her wax must melt. |
| LADY GRAY | (to [[King]] Edward)<br>Why stops my lord? Shall I not hear my task? |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | An easy task—’tis but to [[Love\|love]] a [[King\|king]]. |
| LADY GRAY | That’s soon performed, because I am a subject. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Why, then, thy husband’s lands I freely give thee. 55 |
| LADY GRAY | (curtsies)<br>I take my leave, with many thousand thanks. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to George)<br>The match is made—she seals it with a curtsy. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Lady Gray)<br>But stay thee—’tis the fruits of [[Love\|love]] I mean. |
| LADY GRAY | The fruits of [[Love\|love]] I mean, my loving liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Ay, but I [[Fear\|fear]] me in another sense.<br>What [[Love\|love]] think’st thou I sue so much to get? |
| LADY GRAY | My [[Love\|love]] till [[Death\|death]], my humble thanks, my prayers—<br>That [[Love\|love]] which virtue begs and virtue grants. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | No, by my troth, I did not mean such [[Love\|love]]. |
| LADY GRAY | Why, then, you mean not as I thought you did. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | But now you partly may perceive my mind. |
| LADY GRAY | My mind will never grant what I perceive<br>Your highness aims at, if I aim aright. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | To tell thee plain, I aim to lie with thee. |
| LADY GRAY | To tell you plain, I had rather lie in [[Prison\|prison]]. 70 |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Why, then, thou shalt not have thy husband’s lands. |
| LADY GRAY | Why, then, mine honesty shall be my dower;<br>For by that loss I will not purchase them. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Therein thou wrong’st thy children mightily. |
| LADY GRAY | Herein your highness wrongs both them and me.<br>But, mighty lord, this merry inclination<br>Accords not with the sadness of my suit.<br>Please you dismiss me either with ay or no. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Ay, if thou wilt say ‘ay’ to my request;<br>No, if thou dost say ‘no’ to my demand. |
| LADY GRAY | Then, no, my lord—my suit is at an end. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to George)<br>The widow likes him not—she knits her brows. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | He is the bluntest wooer in Christendom. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>Her looks doth argue her replete with modesty;<br>Her words doth show her wit incomparable;<br>All her perfections challenge sovereignty.<br>One way or other, she is for a [[King\|king]];<br>And she shall be my [[Love\|love]] or else my [[Queen\|queen]].<br>(To Lady Gray) Say that [[King]] Edward take thee for his<br>[[Queen\|queen]]? |
| LADY GRAY | ’Tis better said than done, my gracious lord.<br>I am a subject fit to jest withal,<br>But far unfit to be a sovereign. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Sweet widow, by my state I swear to thee<br>I speak no more than what my [[Soul\|soul]] intends,<br>And that is to enjoy thee for my [[Love\|love]]. |
| LADY GRAY | And that is more than I will yield unto.<br>I know I am too mean to be your [[Queen\|queen]],<br>And yet too good to be your concubine. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | You cavil, widow-I did mean my [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| LADY GRAY | ’Twill grieve your grace my sons should call you father. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | No more than when my daughters call thee mother.<br>Thou art a widow and thou hast some children;<br>And, by God’s mother, I, being but a bachelor,<br>Have other some. Why, ’tis a happy thing<br>To be the father unto many sons.<br>Answer no more, for thou shalt be my [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to George)<br>The ghostly father now hath done his shrift. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | When he was made a shriver, ’twas for shift. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (to [[Richard]] and George)<br>Brothers, you muse what chat we two have had. [[Richard]] and George come forward |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | The widow likes it not, for she looks very sad. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | You’d think it strange if I should marry her. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | To who, my lord ? |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Why, [[Clarence]], to myself. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | That would be ten days’ [[Wonder\|wonder]] at the least. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | That’s a day longer than a [[Wonder\|wonder]] lasts. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | By so much is the [[Wonder\|wonder]] in extremes. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | [[Well]], jest on, brothers—I can tell you both<br>Her suit is granted for her husband’s lands. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Nobleman |
| NOBLEMAN | My gracious lord, Henry your foe is taken<br>And brought as prisoner to your palace gate. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | See that he be conveyed unto the Tower—<br>(To [[Richard]] and George)<br><br>And go we, brothers, to the man that took him,<br>To question of his apprehension. |
| *(didascalia)* | (To Lady Gray) Widow, go you along. To [[Richard]] and |
| *(didascalia)* | George Lords, use her honourably. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[Richard]] |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF GLOUCEST’ER | Ay, Edward will use women honourably.<br>Would he were wasted, marrow, bones, and all,<br>That from his loins no hopeful branch may [[spring]]<br>To [[Cross\|cross]] me from the golden [[Time\|time]] I look for.<br>And yet, between my [[Soul\|soul]]’s [[Desire\|desire]] and me—<br>The lustful Edward’s title burièd—<br>Is [[Clarence]], Henry, and his son young Edward,<br>And all the unlooked-for issue of their bodies,<br>To take their rooms ere I can place myself.<br>A cold premeditation for my purpose.<br>Why, then, I do but dream on sovereignty<br>Like one that stands upon a promontory<br>And spies a far-off shore where he would tread,<br>Wishing his foot were equal with his eye,<br>And chides the sea that sunders him from thence,<br>Saying he’ll lade it dry to have his way—<br>So do I wish the [[Crown\|crown]] being so far off,<br>And so I chide the means that keeps me from it,<br>And so I say I’ll cut the causes off,<br>Flattering me with impossibilities.<br>My eye’s too quick, my heart o‘erweens too much,<br>Unless my hand and strength could equal them.<br>[[Well]], say there is no kingdom then for [[Richard]]—<br>What other pleasure can the world afford?<br>I’ll make my heaven in a lady’s lap,<br>And deck my [[Body\|body]] in gay ornaments,<br>And ’[[Witch\|witch]] sweet ladies with my words and looks.<br>O, miserable thought! And more unlikely<br>Than to accomplish twenty golden crowns.<br>Why, [[Love\|love]] forswore me in my mother’s womb,<br>And, for I should not deal in her soft laws,<br>She did corrupt frail [[Nature\|nature]] with some bribe<br>To shrink mine arm up like a withered shrub,<br>To make an envious mountain on my back—<br>Where sits deformity to mock my [[Body\|body]]—<br>To shape my legs of an unequal size,<br>To disproportion me in every part,<br>Like to a chaos, or an unlicked bear whelp<br>That carries no impression like the dam.<br>And am I then a man to be beloved?<br>O, monstrous fault, to harbour such a thought!<br>Then, since this earth affords no joy to me<br>But to command, to check, to o‘erbear such<br>As are of better person than myself,<br>I’ll make my heaven to dream upon the [[Crown\|crown]],<br>And whiles I live, t’account this world but hell,<br>Until my misshaped trunk that bears this head<br>Be round impaled with a glorious [[Crown\|crown]].<br>And yet I know not how to get the [[Crown\|crown]],<br>For many lives stand between me and [[Home\|home]].<br>And I—like one lost in a thorny wood,<br>That rends the thorns and is rent with the thorns,<br>Seeking a way and straying from the way,<br>Not knowing how to find the open air,<br>But toiling desperately to find it out—<br>Torment myself to catch the English [[Crown\|crown]].<br>And from that torment I will free myself,<br>Or hew my way out with a bloody axe.<br>Why, I can smile, and murder whiles I smile,<br>And cry ‘Content!’ to that which grieves my heart,<br>And wet my cheeks with artificial [[Tears\|tears]],<br>And frame my face to all occasions.<br>I’ll drown more sailors than the [[Mermaid\|mermaid]] shall;<br>I’ll slay more gazers than the basilisk;<br>I’ll play the orator as [[Well\|well]] as Nestor,<br>Deceive more slyly than [[Ulysses]] could,<br>And, like a Sinon, take another Troy.<br>I can add colours to the chameleon,<br>Change shapes with [[Proteus]] for advantages,<br>And set the murderous Machiavel to school.<br>Can I do this, and cannot get a [[Crown\|crown]]?<br>Tut, were it farther off, I’ll pluck it down. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | 3.3 Two chairs of state. Flourish. Enter [[King]] Louis of France, his sister the Lady Bona, Lord Bourbon his admiral, Prince Edward, [[Queen Margaret]], and the Earl of [[Oxford]]. Louis goes up upon the state, sits, and riseth up again |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Fair [[Queen]] of England, worthy Margaret,<br>Sit down with us. It ill befits thy state<br>And birth that thou shouldst stand while Louis<br>doth sit. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | No, mighty [[King of France]], now Margaret<br>Must strike her sail and learn a while to serve<br>Where kings command. I was, I must confess,<br>Great Albion’s [[Queen\|queen]] in former golden days,<br>But now mischance hath trod my title down,<br>And with dishonour laid me on the ground,<br>Where I must take like seat unto my fortune<br>And to my humble state conform myself. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Why, say, fair [[Queen]], whence springs this deep [[Despair\|despair]] ? |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | From such a cause as fills mine eyes with [[Tears\|tears]]<br>And stops my tongue, while heart is drowned in cares. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Whate’er it be, be thou still like thyself,<br>And sit thee by our side.<br>Seats her by him<br>Yield not thy neck<br>To fortune’s yoke, but let thy dauntless mind<br>Still ride in triumph over all mischance.<br>Be plain, [[Queen Margaret]], and tell thy grief.<br>It shall be eased if France can yield relief. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Those gracious words revive my drooping thoughts,<br>And give my tongue-tied sorrows leave to speak.<br>Now, therefore, be it known to noble Louis<br>That Henry, sole possessor of my [[Love\|love]],<br>Is of a [[King\|king]] become a banished man,<br>And forced to live in Scotland a forlorn,<br>While proud ambitious Edward, [[Duke of York]],<br>Usurps the regal title and the seat<br>Of England’s true-anointed lawful [[King]].<br>This is the cause that I, poor Margaret,<br>With this my son, Prince Edward, Henry’s heir,<br>Am come to crave thy just and lawful aid.<br>An if thou fail us all our [[Hope\|hope]] is done.<br>Scotland hath will to help, but cannot help;<br>Our people and our peers are both misled,<br>Our treasure seized, our soldiers put to flight,<br>And, as thou seest, ourselves in heavy plight. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Renowned [[Queen]], with patience calm the [[Storm\|storm]],<br>While we bethink a means to break it off. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | The more we stay, the stronger grows our foe. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | The more I stay, the more I’ll succour thee. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | O, but impatience waiteth on true sorrow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]] |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | And see where comes the breeder of my sorrow. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | What’s he approacheth boldly to our presence? |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Our [[Earl of Warwick]], Edward’s greatest friend. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Welcome, brave Warwick. What brings thee to France ?<br>He descends. She ariseth |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | (aside)<br>Ay, now begins a second [[Storm\|storm]] to rise,<br>For this is he that moves both [[Wind\|wind]] and tide. |
| WARWICK | (to [[King]] Louis)<br>From worthy Edward, [[King]] of Albion,<br>My lord and sovereign, and thy vowed friend,<br>I come in kindness and unfeigned [[Love\|love]],<br>First, to do greetings to thy royal person,<br>And then, to crave a league of amity,<br>And lastly, to confirm that amity<br>With nuptial knot, if thou vouchsafe to grant<br>That virtuous Lady Bona, thy fair sister,<br>To England’s [[King]] in lawful [[Marriage\|marriage]]. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | (aside)<br>If that go forward, Henry’s [[Hope\|hope]] is done. |
| WARWICK | (to Lady Bona)<br>And, gracious madam, in our [[King]]’s behalf<br>I am commanded, with your leave and favour,<br>Humbly to kiss your hand, and with my tongue<br>To tell the passion of my sovereign’s heart,<br>Where fame, late ent‘ring at his heedful ears,<br>Hath placed thy [[Beauty\|beauty]]’s image and thy virtue. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | [[King]] Louis and Lady Bona, hear me speak<br>Before you answer Warwick. His demand<br>Springs not from Edward’s [[Well\|well]]-meant honest [[Love\|love]],<br>But from deceit, bred by necessity.<br>For how can tyrants safely govern [[Home\|home]]<br>Unless abroad they purchase great alliance ?<br>To prove him [[Tyrant\|tyrant]] this reason may suffice—<br>That Henry liveth still; but were he dead,<br>Yet here Prince Edward stands, [[King]] Henry’s son.<br>Look, therefore, Louis, that by this league and<br>[[Marriage\|marriage]]<br>Thou draw not on thy danger and dishonour,<br>For though usurpers sway the rule a while,<br>Yet heav’ns are just and [[Time\|time]] suppresseth wrongs. |
| WARWICK | Injurious Margaret. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | And why not ‘[[Queen]]’? |
| WARWICK | Because thy father Henry did usurp,<br>And thou no more art prince than she is [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | Then Warwick disannuls great [[John of Gaunt]],<br>Which did subdue the greatest part of Spain;<br>And, after [[John of Gaunt]], Henry the Fourth,<br>Whose wisdom was a [[Mirror\|mirror]] to the wisest;<br>And, after that wise prince, Henry the Fifth,<br>Who by his prowess conquered all France.<br>From these our Henry lineally descends. |
| WARWICK | [[Oxford]], how haps it in this smooth discourse<br>You told not how Henry the Sixth hath lost<br>All that which Henry the Fifth had gotten ?<br>Methinks these peers of France should smile at that.<br>But for the rest, you tell a pedigree<br>Of threescore and two years—a silly [[Time\|time]]<br>To make prescription for a kingdom’s worth. |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | Why, Warwick, canst thou speak against thy liege,<br>Whom thou obeyedest thirty and six years,<br>And not bewray thy treason with a blush? |
| WARWICK | Can [[Oxford]], that did ever fence the right,<br>Now buckler falsehood with a pedigree?<br>For shame—leave Henry, and call Edward [[King\|king]]. |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | Call him my [[King\|king]] by whose injurious doom<br>My elder brother, the Lord Aubrey Vere,<br>Was done to [[Death\|death]] ? And more than so, my father,<br>Even in the downfall of his mellowed years,<br>When [[Nature\|nature]] brought him to the door of [[Death\|death]]?<br>No, Warwick, no—while life upholds this arm,<br>This arm upholds the [[house]] of Lancaster. |
| WARWICK | And I the [[house]] of York. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | [[Queen Margaret]], Prince Edward, and [[Oxford]],<br>Vouchsafe, at our request, to stand aside<br>While I use further conference with Warwick. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Queen Margaret]] comes down from the state and, with Prince Edward and [[Oxford]], stands apart |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Heavens grant that Warwick’s words bewitch him not. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Now, Warwick, tell me even upon thy [[Conscience\|conscience]],<br>Is Edward your true [[King\|king]]? For I were loath<br>To link with him that were not lawful chosen. |
| WARWICK | Thereon I pawn my credit and mine [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | But is he gracious in the people’s eye? |
| WARWICK | The more that Henry was unfortunate. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Then further, all dissembling set aside,<br>Tell me for [[Truth\|truth]] the measure of his [[Love\|love]]<br>Unto our sister Bona. |
| WARWICK | Such it seems<br>As may beseem a monarch like himself.<br>Myself have often heard him say and swear<br>That this his [[Love\|love]] was an eternal plant,<br>Whereof the root was fixed in virtue’s ground,<br>The [[Leaves\|leaves]] and fruit maintained with [[Beauty\|beauty]]’s sun,<br>Exempt from envy, but not from disdain,<br>Unless the Lady Bona quit his [[Pain\|pain]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | (to Lady Boa)<br>Now, sister, let us hear your firm resolve. |
| LADY BONA | Your grant, or your denial, shall be mine.<br>(To Warwick) Yet I confess that often ere this day,<br>When I have heard your [[King\|king]]’s [[Desert\|desert]] recounted,<br>Mine ear hath tempted judgement to [[Desire\|desire]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | (to Warwick)<br>Then, Warwick, thus—our sister shall be Edward’s.<br>And now, forthwith, shall articles be drawn<br>Touching the jointure that your [[King\|king]] must make,<br>Which with her dowry shall be counterpoised.<br>(To [[Queen Margaret]]) Draw near, [[Queen Margaret]], and<br>be a witness<br>That Bona shall be wife to the English [[King\|king]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Queen Margaret]], Prince Edward, and [[Oxford]] come forward |
| PRINCE EDWARD | To Edward, but not to the English [[King\|king]]. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Deceitful Warwick—it was thy device<br>By this alliance to make void my suit!<br>Before thy coming Louis was Henry’s friend. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | And still is friend to him and Margaret.<br>But if your title to the [[Crown\|crown]] be weak,<br>As may appear by Edward’s good [[success]],<br>Then ’tis but reason that I be released<br>From giving aid which late I promised.<br>Yet shall you have all kindness at my hand<br>That your estate requires and mine can yield. |
| WARWICK | (to [[Queen Margaret]])<br>Henry now lives in Scotland at his ease,<br>Where having nothing, nothing can he lose.<br>And as for you yourself, our quondam [[Queen\|queen]],<br>You have a father able to maintain you,<br>And better ’twere you troubled him than France. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Peace, impudent and shameless Warwick, peace!<br>Proud setter-up and puller-down of kings!<br>I will not hence till, with my talk and [[Tears\|tears]],<br>Both full of [[Truth\|truth]], I make [[King]] Louis behold<br>Thy sly conveyance and thy lord’s false [[Love\|love]],<br>Post blowing a horn within<br><br>For both of you are [[Birds\|birds]] of selfsame feather. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Warwick, this is some post to us or thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Post |
| POST | (to Warwick)<br>My lord ambassador, these letters are for you,<br>Sent from your brother Marquis Montague;<br>(To Louis) These from our [[King]] unto your majesty;<br>(To [[Queen Margaret]])<br>And, madam, these for you, from whom I know not.<br>They all read their letters |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | (to Prince Edward)<br>I like it [[Well\|well]] that our fair [[Queen]] and mistress<br>Smiles at her news, while Warwick frowns at his. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | Nay, mark how Louis stamps as he were nettled.<br>I [[Hope\|hope]] all’s for the best. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Warwick, what are thy news ? And yours, fair [[Queen]]? |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Mine, such as fill my heart with unhoped joys. |
| WARWICK | Mine, full of sorrow and heart’s discontent. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | What! Has your [[King\|king]] married the Lady Gray?<br>And now to soothe your forgery and his,<br>Sends me a paper to persuade me patience?<br>Is this th’alliance that he seeks with France?<br>Dare he presume to scorn us in this manner? |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | I told your majesty as much before—<br>This proveth Edward’s [[Love\|love]] and Warwick’s honesty. |
| WARWICK | [[King]] Louis, I here protest in sight of heaven<br>And by the [[Hope\|hope]] I have of heavenly bliss,<br>That I am clear from this misdeed of Edward‘s,<br>No more my [[King\|king]], for he dishonours me,<br>But most himself, if he could see his shame.<br>Did I forget that by the [[house]] of York<br>My father came untimely to his [[Death\|death]]?<br>Did I let pass th’abuse done to my niece?<br>Did I impale him with the regal [[Crown\|crown]]?<br>Did I put Henry from his native right?<br>And am I guerdoned at the last with shame?<br>Shame on himself, for my [[Desert\|desert]] is [[Honour\|honour]].<br>And to repair my [[Honour\|honour]], lost for him,<br>I here renounce him and return to Henry.<br>(To [[Queen Margaret]]) My noble [[Queen]], let former<br>grudges pass,<br>And henceforth I am thy true servitor.<br>I will [[Revenge\|revenge]] his wrong to Lady Bona<br>And replant Henry in his former state. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Warwick, these words have turned my hate to [[Love\|love]],<br>And I forgive and quite forget old faults,<br>And joy that thou becom’st [[King]] Henry’s friend. |
| WARWICK | So much his friend, ay, his unfeigned friend,<br>That if [[King]] Louis vouchsafe to furnish us<br>With some few bands of chosen soldiers,<br>I’ll undertake to land them on our coast<br>And force the [[Tyrant\|tyrant]] from his seat by war.<br>’Tis not his new-made bride shall succour him.<br>And as for [[Clarence]], as my letters tell me,<br>He’s very likely now to fall from him<br>For matching more for wanton lust than [[Honour\|honour]],<br>Or than for strength and safety of our country. |
| LADY BONA | (to [[King]] Louis)<br>Dear brother, how shall Bona be revenged,<br>But by thy help to this distressed [[Queen]]? |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | (to [[King]] Louis)<br>Renowned Prince, how shall poor Henry live<br>Unless thou rescue him from foul [[Despair\|despair]]? |
| LADY BONA | (to [[King]] Louis)<br>My quarrel and this English [[Queen]]’s are one. |
| WARWICK | And mine, fair Lady Bona, joins with yours. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | And mine with hers, and thine, and Margaret’s.<br>Therefore at last I firmly am resolved:<br>You shall have aid. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Let me give humble thanks for all at once. |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | (to the Post)<br>Then, England’s messenger, return in post<br>And tell false Edward, thy supposed [[King\|king]],<br>That Louis of France is sending over masquers<br>To revel it with him and his new bride.<br>Thou seest what’s passed, go [[Fear\|fear]] thy [[King\|king]] withal. |
| LADY BONA | (to the Post)<br>Tell him, in [[Hope\|hope]] he’ll prove a widower shortly,<br>I’ll wear the willow garland for his sake. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | (to the Post)<br>Tell him my mourning weeds are laid aside,<br>And I am ready to put armour on. |
| WARWICK | (to the Post)<br>Tell him from me that he hath done me wrong,<br>And therefore I’ll uncrown him ere’t be long.<br>(Giving money) There’s thy reward—be gone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Post |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | But, Warwick, thou and [[Oxford]], with five thousand<br>men,<br>Shall [[Cross\|cross]] the seas and bid false Edward battle;<br>And, as occasion serves, this noble [[Queen]]<br>And Prince shall follow with a fresh supply.<br>Yet, ere thou go, but answer me one [[Doubt\|doubt]]:<br>What pledge have we of thy firm loyalty? |
| WARWICK | This shall assure my constant loyalty :<br>That if our [[Queen]] and this young Prince agree,<br>I’ll join mine eldest daughter and my joy<br>To him forthwith in holy wedlock bands. |
| [[Queen Margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Yes, I agree, and thank you for your motion.<br>(To Prince Edward) Son Edward, she is fair and virtuous,<br>Therefore delay not. Give thy hand to Warwick,<br>And with thy hand thy [[Faith\|faith]] irrevocable<br>That only Warwick’s daughter shall be thine. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | Yes, I accept her, for she [[Well\|well]] deserves it,<br>And here to pledge my vow I give my hand.<br>He and Warwick clasp hands |
| [[King\|KING]] LOUIS | Why stay we now? These soldiers shall be levied,<br>And thou, Lord Bourbon, our high admiral,<br>Shall waft them over with our royal fleet.<br>I long till Edward fall by war’s mischance<br>For mocking [[Marriage\|marriage]] with a dame of France. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Warwick |
| WARWICK | I came from Edward as ambassador,<br>But I return his sworn and mortal foe.<br>Matter of [[Marriage\|marriage]] was the charge he gave me,<br>But dreadful war shall answer his demand.<br>Had he none else to make a stale but me?<br>Then none but I shall turn his jest to sorrow.<br>I was the chief that raised him to the [[Crown\|crown]],<br>And I’ll be chief to bring him down again.<br>Not that I pity Henry’s misery,<br>But seek [[Revenge\|revenge]] on Edward’s mockery. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]], George Duke of [[Clarence]], the Duke of Somerset, and the Marquis of Montague |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Now tell me, brother [[Clarence]], what think you<br>Of this new [[Marriage\|marriage]] with the Lady Gray?<br>Hath not our brother made a worthy choice ? |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Alas, you know ’tis far from hence to France;<br>How could he stay till Warwick made return? |
| SOMERSET | My lords, forbear this talk—here comes the [[King]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter [[King]] Edward, the Lady Gray his |
| SOMERSET | [[Queen]], the Earl of Pembroke, and the Lords |
| *(didascalia)* | Stafford and [[Hastings]]. Four stand on one side of |
| *(didascalia)* | the [[King]], and four on the other |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And his [[Well\|well]]-chosen bride. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | I mind to tell him plainly what I think. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, brother of [[Clarence]], how like you our choice,<br>That you stand pensive, as half-malcontent? |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | As [[Well\|well]] as Louis of France, or the [[Earl of Warwick]],<br>Which are so weak of [[Courage\|courage]] and in judgement<br>That they’ll take no offence at our abuse. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Suppose they take offence without a cause—<br>They are but Louis and Warwick; I am Edward,<br>Your [[King\|king]] and Warwick’s, and must have my will. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And you shall have your will, because our [[King\|king]].<br>Yet hasty [[Marriage\|marriage]] seldom proveth [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Yea, brother [[Richard]], are you offended too ? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Not I, no—God forbid that I should wish them severed<br>Whom God hath joined together. Ay, and ’twere pity<br>To sunder them that yoke so [[Well\|well]] together. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Setting your scorns and your mislike aside,<br>Tell me some reason why the Lady Gray<br>Should not become my wife and England’s [[Queen\|queen]].<br>And you too, Somerset and Montague,<br>Speak freely what you think. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Then this is my opinion: that [[King]] Louis<br>Becomes your enemy for mocking him<br>About the [[Marriage\|marriage]] of the Lady Bona. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And Warwick, doing what you gave in charge,<br>Is now dishonoured by this new [[Marriage\|marriage]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | What if both Louis and Warwick be appeased<br>By such invention as I can devise? |
| MONTAGUE | Yet, to have joined with France in such alliance<br>Would more have strengthened this our<br>commonwealth<br>’Gainst foreign storms than any [[Home\|home]]-bred [[Marriage\|marriage]]. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Why, knows not Montague that of itself<br>England is safe, if true within itself? |
| MONTAGUE | But the safer when ’tis backed with France. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | ’Tis better using France than trusting France.<br>Let us be backed with God and with the seas<br>Which he hath giv’n for fence impregnable,<br>And with their helps only defend ourselves.<br>In them and in ourselves our safety lies. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | For this one speech Lord [[Hastings]] [[Well\|well]] deserves<br>To have the heir of the Lord Hungerford. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Ay, what of that? It was my will and grant—<br>And for this once my will shall stand for law. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And yet, methinks, your grace hath not done [[Well\|well]]<br>To give the heir and daughter of Lord Scales<br>Unto the brother of your loving bride.<br>She better would have fitted me or [[Clarence]],<br>But in your bride you bury brotherhood. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Or else you would not have bestowed the heir<br>Of the Lord Bonville on your new wife’s son,<br>And leave your brothers to go [[Speed\|speed]] elsewhere. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Alas, poor [[Clarence]], is it for a wife<br>That thou art malcontent? I will provide thee. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | In choosing for yourself you showed your judgement,<br>Which being shallow, you shall give me leave<br>To play the broker in mine own behalf,<br>And to that end I shortly mind to leave you. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Leave me, or tarry. Edward will be [[King\|king]],<br>And not be tied unto his brother’s will. |
| LADY GRAY | My lords, before it pleased his majesty<br>To raise my state to title of a [[Queen\|queen]],<br>Do me but right, and you must all confess<br>That I was not ignoble of descent—<br>And meaner than myself have had like fortune.<br>But as this title honours me and mine,<br>So your dislikes, to whom I would be pleasing,<br>Doth cloud my joys with danger and with sorrow. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | My [[Love\|love]], forbear to fawn upon their frowns.<br>What danger or what sorrow can befall thee<br>So long as Edward is thy constant friend,<br>And their true sovereign, whom they must obey?<br>Nay, whom they shall obey, and [[Love\|love]] thee too—<br>Unless they seek for hatred at my hands,<br>Which if they do, yet will I keep thee safe,<br>And they shall feel the vengeance of my wrath. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside)<br>I hear, yet say not much, but think the more. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Post from France |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, messenger, what letters or what news from France ? |
| POST | My sovereign liege, no letters and few words,<br>But such as I, without your special pardon,<br>Dare not relate. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Go to, we pardon thee. Therefore, in brief,<br>Tell me their words as near as thou canst guess them.<br>What answer makes [[King]] Louis unto our letters? |
| POST | At my depart these were his very words:<br>‘Go tell false Edward, thy supposed [[King\|king]],<br>That Louis of France is sending over masquers<br>To revel it with him and his new bride.’ |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Is Louis so brave ? Belike he thinks me Henry.<br>But what said Lady Bona to my [[Marriage\|marriage]]? |
| POST | These were her words, uttered with mild disdain:<br>‘Tell him in [[Hope\|hope]] he’ll prove a widower shortly,<br>I’ll wear the willow garland for his sake.’ |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | ame not her, she could say little less;<br>She had the wrong. But what said Henry’s [[Queen\|queen]]?<br>For I have heard that she was there in place. |
| POST | ‘Tell him‘, quoth she, ’my mourning weeds are done,<br>And I am ready to put armour on.’ |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Belike she minds to play the Amazon.<br>But what said Warwick to these injuries? |
| POST | He, more incensed against your majesty<br>Than all the rest, discharged me with these words:<br>‘Tell him from me that he hath done me wrong,<br>And therefore I’ll uncrown him ere’t be long.’ |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Ha! Durst the traitor breathe out so proud words?<br>[[Well]], I will arm me, being thus forewarned.<br>They shall have wars and pay for their presumption.<br>But say, is Warwick friends with Margaret? |
| POST | Ay, gracious sovereign, they are so linked in [[Friendship\|friendship]]<br>That young Prince Edward marries Warwick’s daughter. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Belike the elder; [[Clarence]] will have the younger.<br>Now, brother [[King]], farewell, and sit you fast,<br>For I will hence to Warwick’s other daughter,<br>That, though I want a kingdom, yet in [[Marriage\|marriage]]<br>I may not prove inferior to yourself.<br>You that [[Love\|love]] me and Warwick, follow me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Clarence]], and Somerset follows |
| *(didascalia)* | Not I—aside my thoughts aim at a further matter. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I stay not for the [[Love\|love]] of Edward, but the [[Crown\|crown]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | [[Clarence]] and Somerset both gone to Warwick?<br>Yet am I armed against the worst can happen,<br>And haste is needful in this desp’rate case.<br>Pembroke and Stafford, you in our behalf<br>Go levy men and make prepare for war.<br>They are already, or quickly will be, landed.<br>Myself in person will straight follow you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Pembroke and Stafford |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | But ere I go, [[Hastings]] and Montague,<br>Resolve my [[Doubt\|doubt]]. You twain, of all the rest,<br>Are near’st to Warwick by [[Blood\|blood]] and by alliance.<br>Tell me if you [[Love\|love]] Warwick more than me.<br>If it be so, then both depart to him—<br>I rather wish you foes than hollow friends.<br>But if you mind to hold your true obedience,<br>Give me assurance with some friendly vow<br>That I may never have you in suspect. |
| MONTAGUE | So God help Montague as he proves true. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | And [[Hastings]] as he favours Edward’s cause. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, brother [[Richard]], will you stand by us? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Ay, in despite of all that shall withstand you. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Why, so. Then am I sure of victory.<br>Now, therefore, let us hence and lose no hour<br>Till we meet Warwick with his foreign power. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earls of Warwick and [[Oxford]] in England, with French soldiers |
| WARWICK | Trust me, my lord, all hitherto goes [[Well\|well]].<br>The common sort by numbers swarm to us. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Dukes of [[Clarence]] and Somerset |
| WARWICK | But see where Somerset and [[Clarence]] comes.<br>Speak suddenly, my lords, are we all friends ? |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | [[Fear]] not that, my lord. |
| WARWICK | Then, gentle [[Clarence]], welcome unto Warwick—<br>And welcome, Somerset. I hold it cowardice<br>To rest mistrustful where a noble heart<br>Hath pawned an open hand in sign of [[Love\|love]],<br>Else might I think that [[Clarence]], Edward’s brother,<br>Were but a feigned friend to our proceedings.<br>But come, sweet [[Clarence]], my daughter shall be thine.<br>And now what rests but, in [[Night\|night]]’s coverture,<br>Thy brother being carelessly encamped,<br>His soldiers lurking in the towns about,<br>And but attended by a simple guard,<br>We may surprise and take him at our pleasure?<br>Our scouts have found the adventure very easy;<br>That, as [[Ulysses]] and stout Diomed<br>With sleight and manhood stole to Rhesus’ tents<br>And brought from thence the Thracian fatal steeds,<br>So we, [[Well\|well]] covered with the [[Night\|night]]’s black mantle,<br>At unawares may beat down Edward’s guard<br>And seize himself—I say not ‘slaughter him’,<br>For I intend but only to surprise him.<br>You that will follow me to this attempt,<br>Applaud the name of Henry with your leader.<br>They all cry ‘Henry’<br>Why, then, let’s on our way in silent sort,<br>For Warwick and his friends, God and Saint George! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter three Watchmen, to guard [[King]] Edward’s tent |
| FIRST WATCHMAN | Come on, my masters, each man take his stand.<br>The [[King]] by this is set him down to [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| SECOND WATCHMAN | What, will he not to bed? |
| FIRST WATCHMAN | Why, no—for he hath made a solemn vow<br>Never to lie and take his natural rest<br>Till Warwick or himself be quite suppressed. |
| SECOND WATCHMAN | Tomorrow then belike shall be the day,<br>If Warwick be so near as men report. |
| THIRD WATCHMAN | But say, I pray, what nobleman is that<br>That with the [[King]] here resteth in his tent? |
| FIRST WATCHMAN | ’Tis the Lord [[Hastings]], the [[King]]’s chiefest friend. |
| THIRD WATCHMAN | O, is it so ? But why commands the [[King]]<br>That his chief followers lodge in towns about him,<br>While he himself keeps in the cold field? |
| SECOND WATCHMAN | ’Tis the more [[Honour\|honour]], because more dangerous. |
| THIRD WATCHMAN | Ay, but give me worship and quietness—<br>I like it better than a dangerous [[Honour\|honour]].<br>If Warwick knew in what estate he stands,<br>’Tis to be doubted he would waken him. |
| FIRST WATCHMAN | Unless our halberds did shut up his passage. |
| SECOND WATCHMAN | Ay, wherefore else guard we his royal tent<br>But to defend his person from [[Night\|night]]-foes? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter silently the [[Earl of Warwick]], George Duke of |
| SECOND WATCHMAN | [[Clarence]], the Earl of [[Oxford]], and the Duke of<br><br>Somerset, with French soldiers |
| WARWICK | This is his tent—and see where stand his guard.<br>[[Courage]], my masters—[[Honour\|honour]] now or never!<br>But follow me, and Edward shall be ours. |
| FIRST WATCHMAN | Who goes there? |
| SECOND WATCHMAN | Stay or thou diest.<br>Warwick and the rest all cry ‘Warwick, Warwick!’<br><br>and set upon the guard, who fly, crying ‘Arm, arm!’<br><br>Warwick and the rest follow them |

### Atto 4, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | With the drummer playing and trumpeter sounding, enter the [[Earl of Warwick]], the Duke of Somerset, and the rest bringing [[King]] Edward out in his gown, sitting in a chair. [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]] and Lord [[Hastings]] flies over the stage |
| SOMERSET | What are they that fly there? |
| WARWICK | [[Richard]] and [[Hastings]]—let them go. Here is the Duke. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | ‘The Duke’! Why, Warwick, when we parted,<br>Thou calledst me [[King\|king]]. |
| WARWICK | Ay, but the case is altered.<br>When you disgraced me in my embassade,<br>Then I degraded you from being [[King\|king]],<br>And come now to create you [[Duke of York]].<br>Alas, how should you govern any kingdom<br>That know not how to use ambassadors,<br>Nor how to be contented with one wife,<br>Nor how to use your brothers brotherly,<br>Nor how to study for the people’s welfare,<br>Nor how to shroud yourself from enemies? |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | (seeing George)<br>Yea, brother of [[Clarence]], art thou here too?<br>Nay, then, I see that Edward needs must down.<br>Yet, Warwick, in despite of all mischance,<br>Of thee thyself and all thy complices,<br>Edward will always bear himself as [[King\|king]].<br>Though fortune’s malice overthrow my state,<br>My mind exceeds the compass of her [[Wheel\|wheel]]. |
| WARWICK | Then, for his mind, be Edward England’s [[King\|king]].<br>Warwick takes off Edward’s [[Crown\|crown]]<br>But Henry now shall wear the English [[Crown\|crown]],<br>And be true [[King\|king]] indeed, thou but the [[Shadow\|shadow]].<br>My lord of Somerset, at my request,<br>See that, forthwith, Duke Edward be conveyed<br>Unto my brother, Archbishop of York.<br>When I have fought with Pembroke and his fellows,<br>I’ll follow you, and tell what answer<br>Louis and the Lady Bona send to him.<br>Now for a while farewell, good [[Duke of York]].<br>They begin to lead Edward out forcibly |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | What fates impose, that men must needs abide.<br>It boots not to resist both [[Wind\|wind]] and tide. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt some with Edward |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | What now remains, my lords, for us to do<br>But march to [[London]] with our soldiers? |
| WARWICK | Ay, that’s the first thing that we have to do—<br>To free [[King]] Henry from [[Imprisonment\|imprisonment]]<br>And see him seated in the regal throne. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Earl Rivers and his sister, Lady Gray, Edward’s [[Queen\|queen]] |
| RIVERS | Madam, what makes you in this sudden change? |
| LADY GRAY | Why, brother Rivers, are you yet to learn<br>What late misfortune is befall’n [[King]] Edward? |
| RIVERS | What? Loss of some pitched battle against Warwick? |
| LADY GRAY | No, but the loss of his own royal person. |
| RIVERS | Then is my sovereign slain? |
| LADY GRAY | Ay, almost stain—for he is taken prisoner,<br>Either betrayed by falsehood of his guard<br>Or by his foe surprised at unawares,<br>And, as I further have to understand,<br>Is new committed to the Bishop of York,<br>Fell Warwick’s brother, and by that our foe. |
| RIVERS | These news, I must confess, are full of grief.<br>Yet, gracious madam, bear it as you may.<br>Warwick may lose, that now hath won the day. |
| LADY GRAY | Till then fair [[Hope\|hope]] must hinder life’s [[Decay\|decay]],<br>And I the rather wean me from [[Despair\|despair]]<br>For [[Love\|love]] of Edward’s offspring in my womb.<br>This is it that makes me bridle passion<br>And bear with mildness my misfortune’s [[Cross\|cross]].<br>Ay, ay, for this I draw in many a tear<br>And stop the rising of [[Blood\|blood]]-sucking sighs,<br>Lest with my sighs or [[Tears\|tears]] I blast or drown<br>[[King]] Edward’s fruit, true heir to th’English [[Crown\|crown]]. |
| RIVERS | But, madam, where is Warwick then become? |
| LADY GRAY | I am informed that he comes towards [[London]]<br>To set the [[Crown\|crown]] once more on Henry’s head.<br>Guess thou the rest—[[King]] Edward’s friends must down.<br>But to prevent the [[Tyrant\|tyrant]]’s violence—<br>For trust not him that hath once broken [[Faith\|faith]]—<br>I’ll hence forthwith unto the sanctuary,<br>To save at least the heir of Edward’s right.<br>There shall I rest secure from force and fraud.<br>Come, therefore, let us fly while we may fly.<br>If Warwick take us, we are sure to die. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]], Lord [[Hastings]], and Sir William Stanley, with soldiers |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Now my lord [[Hastings]] and Sir William Stanley,<br>Leave off to [[Wonder\|wonder]] why I drew you hither<br>Into this chiefest thicket of the park.<br>Thus stands the case: you know our [[King]], my brother,<br>Is prisoner to the Bishop here, at whose hands<br>He hath good usage and great liberty,<br>And, often but attended with weak guard,<br>Comes hunting this way to disport himself.<br>I have advertised him by secret means<br>That if about this hour he make this way<br>Under the colour of his usual game,<br>He shall here find his friends with [[Horse\|horse]] and men<br>To set him free from his captivity. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Edward and a Huntsman with him |
| HUNTSMAN | This way, my lord—for this way lies the game. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Nay, this way, man—see where the huntsmen stand.<br>Now, brother of [[Gloucester]], Lord [[Hastings]], and the<br>rest,<br>Stand you thus close to steal the Bishop’s deer? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Brother, the [[Time\|time]] and case requireth haste.<br>Your [[Horse\|horse]] stands ready at the park corner. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | But whither shall we then? |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | To Lynn, my lord,<br>And shipped from thence to Flanders. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] aside |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | [[Well]] guessed, believe me—for that was my meaning. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Stanley, I will requite thy forwardness. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | But wherefore stay we? ’Tis no [[Time\|time]] to talk. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Huntsman, what sayst thou? Wilt thou go along? |
| HUNTSMAN | Better do so than tarry and be hanged. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Come then, away—let’s have no more ado. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Bishop, farewell—shield thee from Warwick’s frown,<br>And pray that I may repossess the [[Crown\|crown]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]] and George Duke of [[Clarence]] with the [[Crown\|crown]]. Then enter [[King]] Henry, the Earl of [[Oxford]], the Duke of Somerset withl young Henry Ear of Richmond, the Marquis of Montague, and the Lieutenant of the Tower |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Master Lieutenant, now that God and friends<br>Have shaken Edward from the regal seat<br>And turned my captive state to liberty,<br>My [[Fear\|fear]] to [[Hope\|hope]], my sorrows unto joys,<br>At our enlargement what are thy due fees? |
| LIEUTENANT | Subjects may challenge nothing of their sovereigns—<br>But if an humble [[Prayer\|prayer]] may prevail,<br>I then crave pardon of your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | For what, Lieutenant? For [[Well\|well]] using me?<br>Nay, be thou sure I’ll [[Well\|well]] requite thy kindness,<br>For that it made my prisonment a pleasure—<br>Ay, such a pleasure as encagèd [[Birds\|birds]]<br>Conceive when, after many moody thoughts,<br>At last by notes of household harmony<br>They quite forget their loss of liberty.<br>But, Warwick, after God, thou sett’st me free,<br>And chiefly therefore I thank God and thee.<br>He was the author, thou the instrument.<br>Therefore, that I may conquer fortune’s spite<br>By living low, where fortune cannot hurt me,<br>And that the people of this blessed land<br>May not be punished with my thwarting [[Stars\|stars]],<br>Warwick, although my head still wear the [[Crown\|crown]],<br>I here resign my [[government]] to thee,<br>For thou art fortunate in all thy deeds. |
| WARWICK | Your grace hath still been famed for virtuous,<br>And now may seem as wise as virtuous<br>By spying and avoiding fortune’s malice,<br>For few men rightly temper with the [[Stars\|stars]].<br>Yet in this one thing let me blame your grace:<br>For choosing me when [[Clarence]] is in place. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | No, Warwick, thou art worthy of the sway,<br>To whom the heav’ns in thy nativity<br>Adjudged an olive branch and laurel [[Crown\|crown]],<br>As likely to be blest in peace and war.<br>And therefore I yield thee my free consent. |
| WARWICK | And I choose [[Clarence]] only for Protector. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Warwick and [[Clarence]], give me both your hands.<br>Now join your hands, and with your hands your<br>hearts,<br>That no dissension hinder [[government]].<br>I make you both Protectors of this land,<br>While I myself will lead a private life<br>And in devotion spend my latter days,<br>To sin’s rebuke and my creator’s praise. |
| WARWICK | What answers [[Clarence]] to his sovereign’s will? |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | That he consents, if Warwick yield consent,<br>For on thy fortune I repose myself. |
| WARWICK | Why, then, though loath, yet must I be content.<br>We’ll yoke together, like a [[Double\|double]] [[Shadow\|shadow]]<br>To Henry’s [[Body\|body]], and supply his place—<br>I mean in bearing weight of [[government]]—<br>While he enjoys the [[Honour\|honour]] and his ease.<br>And, [[Clarence]], now then it is more than needful<br>Forthwith that Edward be pronounced a traitor,<br>And all his lands and goods be confiscate. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | What else? And that succession be determined. |
| WARWICK | Ay, therein [[Clarence]] shall not want his part. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | But with the first of all your chief affairs,<br>Let me entreat—for I command no more—<br>That Margaret your [[Queen\|queen]] and my son Edward<br>Be sent for, to return from France with [[Speed\|speed]].<br>For, till I see them here, by doubtful [[Fear\|fear]]<br>My joy of liberty is half eclipsed. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | It shall be done, my sovereign, with all [[Speed\|speed]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | My lord of Somerset, what youth is that<br>Of whom you seem to have so tender care? |
| SOMERSET | My liege, it is young Henry, [[Earl of Richmond]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Come hither, England’s [[Hope\|hope]].<br>[[King]] Henry lays his hand on Richmond’s head If secret powers<br>Suggest but [[Truth\|truth]] to my divining thoughts,<br>This pretty lad will prove our country’s bliss.<br>His looks are full of peaceful majesty,<br>His head by [[Nature\|nature]] framed to wear a [[Crown\|crown]],<br>His hand to wield a sceptre, and himself<br>Likely in [[Time\|time]] to bless a regal throne.<br>Make much of him, my lords, for this is he<br>Must help you more than you are hurt by me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Post |
| WARWICK | What news, my friend? |
| POST | That Edward is escaped from your brother<br>And fled, as he hears since, to Burgundy. |
| WARWICK | Unsavoury news—but how made he escape ? |
| POST | He was conveyed by [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]]<br>And the Lord [[Hastings]], who attended him<br>In secret ambush on the forest side<br>And from the Bishop’s huntsmen rescued him—<br>For hunting was his daily exercise. |
| WARWICK | My brother was too careless of his charge.<br>(To [[King]] Henry) But let us hence, my sovereign, to<br>provide<br>A salve for any sore that may betide. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Somerset, Richmond, and [[Oxford]] |
| SOMERSET | (to [[Oxford]])<br>My lord, I like not of this flight of Edward’s,<br>For doubtless Burgundy will yield him help,<br>And we shall have more wars before’t be long.<br>As Henry’s late presaging prophecy<br>Did glad my heart with [[Hope\|hope]] of this young Richmond,<br>So doth my heart misgive me, in these conflicts,<br>What may befall him, to his harm and ours.<br>Therefore, Lord [[Oxford]], to prevent the worst,<br>Forthwith we’ll send him hence to Brittany,<br>Till storms be past of civil enmity. |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | Ay, for if Edward repossess the [[Crown\|crown]],<br>’Tis like that Richmond with the rest shall down. |
| SOMERSET | It shall be so—he shall to Brittany.<br>Come, therefore, let’s about it speedily. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 8

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter [[King]] Edward, [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]], and Lord [[Hastings]], With a troop of Hollanders |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, brother [[Richard]], Lord [[Hastings]], and the rest,<br>Yet thus far fortune maketh us amends,<br>And says that once more I shall interchange<br>My waned state for Henry’s regal [[Crown\|crown]].<br>[[Well]] have we passed and now repassed the seas<br>And brought desired help from Burgundy.<br>What then remains, we being thus arrived<br>From Ravenspurgh haven before the gates of York,<br>But that we enter, as into our dukedom ? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Hastings]] knocks at the gates of York |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | The gates made fast? Brother, I like not this.<br>For many men that stumble at the threshold<br>Are [[Well\|well]] foretold that danger lurks within. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Tush, man, abodements must not now affright us.<br>By fair or foul means we must enter in,<br>For hither will our friends repair to us. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | My liege, I’ll knock once more to summon them.<br>He knocks. Enter, on the walls, the Mayor and aldermen of York |
| MAYOR | My lords, we were forewarned of your coming,<br>And shut the gates for safety of oursetves—<br>For now we owe allegiance unto Henry. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | But, Master Mayor, if Henry be your [[King\|king]],<br>Yet Edward at the least is [[Duke of York]]. |
| MAYOR | True, my good lord, I know you for no less. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Why, and I challenge nothing but my dukedom,<br>As being [[Well\|well]] content with that [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside)<br>But when the fox hath once got in his nose,<br>He’ll soon find means to make the [[Body\|body]] follow. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Why, Master Mayor, why stand you in a [[Doubt\|doubt]]?<br>Open the gates—we are [[King]] Henry’s friends. |
| MAYOR | Ay, say you so? The gates shall then be opened.<br>They descend |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | A wise stout captain, and soon persuaded. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | The good old man would fain that all were [[Well\|well]],<br>So ’twere not long of him; but being entered,<br>I [[Doubt\|doubt]] not, I, but we shall soon persuade<br>Both him and all his brothers unto reason. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter below the Mayor and two aldermen |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | So, Master Mayor, these gates must not be shut<br>But in the [[Night\|night]] or in the [[Time\|time]] of war.<br>What—[[Fear\|fear]] not, man, but yield me up the keys,<br>[[King]] Edward takes some keys from the Mayor<br>For Edward will defend the town and thee,<br>And all those friends that deign to follow me.<br>March. Enter Sir John Montgomery with a drummer and soldiers |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Brother, this is Sir John Montgomery,<br>Our trusty friend, unless I be deceived. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Welcome, Sir John—but why come you in arms? |
| MONTGOMERY | To help [[King]] Edward in his [[Time\|time]] of [[Storm\|storm]],<br>As every loyal subject ought to do. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Thanks, good Montgomery, but we now forget<br>Our title to the [[Crown\|crown]], and only claim<br>Our dukedom till God please to send the rest. |
| MONTGOMERY | Then fare you [[Well\|well]], for I will hence again.<br>I came to serve a [[King\|king]] and not a duke.<br>Drummer, strike up, and let us march away. 50<br>The drummer begins to sound a march |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Nay, stay, Sir John, a while, and we’ll [[debate]]<br>By what safe means the [[Crown\|crown]] may be recovered. |
| MONTGOMERY | What talk you of debating? In few words,<br>If you’ll not here proclaim yourself our [[King\|king]]<br>I’ll leave you to your fortune and be gone<br>To keep them back that come to succour you.<br>Why shall we fight, if you pretend no title? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to [[King]] Edward)<br>Why, brother, wherefore stand you on nice points? |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | When we grow stronger, then we’ll make our claim.<br>Till then ’tis wisdom to conceal our meaning. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Away with scrupulous wit! Now arms must rule. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And fearless minds climb soonest unto crowns.<br>Brother, we will proclaim you out of hand,<br>The bruit thereof will bring you many friends. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Then be it as you will, for ’tis my right,<br>And Henry but usurps the diadem. |
| MONTGOMERY | Ay, now my sovereign speaketh like himself,<br>And now will I be Edward’s champion. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound trumpet, Edward shall be here proclaimed. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Montgomery |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Come, fellow soldier, make thou proclamation. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish |
| *(didascalia)* | MONTGOMERY Edward the Fourth, by the grace of God |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | [[King]] of England and France, and Lord of Ireland—<br>And whosoe’er gainsays [[King]] Edward’s right,<br>By this I challenge him to single fight.<br>He throws down his gauntlet |
| ALL | Long live Edward the Fourth! |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Thanks, brave Montgomery, and thanks unto you all.<br>If fortune serve me I’ll requite this kindness.<br>Now, for this [[Night\|night]], let’s harbour here in York;<br>And when the morning sun shall raise his car<br>Above the border of this horizon,<br>We’ll forward towards Warwick and his mates.<br>For [[Well\|well]] I wot that Henry is no soldier.<br>Ah, froward [[Clarence]], how evil it beseems thee<br>To flatter Henry and forsake thy brother!<br>Yet, as we may, we’ll meet both thee and Warwick.<br>Come on, brave soldiers—[[Doubt\|doubt]] not of the day<br>And, that once gotten, [[Doubt\|doubt]] not of large pay. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 9

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter [[King]] Henry, the [[Earl of Warwick]], the Marquis of Montague, George Duke of [[Clarence]], and the Earl of [[Oxford]] |
| WARWICK | What counsel, lords? Edward from Belgia,<br>With hasty Germans and blunt Hollanders,<br>Hath passed in safety through the narrow seas,<br>And with his troops doth march amain to [[London]],<br>And many giddy people flock to him. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Let’s levy men and beat him back again. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | A little [[Fire\|fire]] is quickly trodden out,<br>Which, being suffered, rivers cannot quench. |
| WARWICK | In Warwickshire I have true-hearted friends,<br>Not mutinous in peace, yet bold in war.<br>Those will I muster up. And thou, son [[Clarence]],<br>Shalt stir in Suffolk, Norfolk, and in [[Kent]],<br>The knights and gentlemen to come with thee.<br>Thou, brother Montague, in [[Buckingham]],<br>Northampton, and in Leicestershire shalt find<br>Men [[Well\|well]] inclined to hear what thou command’st.<br>And thou, brave [[Oxford]], wondrous [[Well\|well]] beloved<br>In Oxfordshire, shalt muster up thy friends.<br>My sovereign, with the loving citizens,<br>Like to his island girt in with the ocean,<br>Or modest Dian circled with her nymphs,<br>Shall rest in [[London]] till we come to him.<br>Fair lords, take leave and stand not to reply.<br>Farewell, my sovereign. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Farewell, my [[Hector]], and my Troy’s true [[Hope\|hope]]. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | In sign of [[Truth\|truth]], I kiss your highness’ hand.<br>He kisses [[King]] Henry’s hand |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | [[Well]]-minded [[Clarence]], be thou fortunate. |
| MONTAGUE | Comfort, my lord, and so I take my leave. |
| *(didascalia)* | He kisses [[King]] Henry’s hand |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | And thus I seal my [[Truth\|truth]] and bid adieu. |
| *(didascalia)* | He kisses [[King]] Henry’s hand |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Sweet [[Oxford]], and my loving Montague,<br>And all at once, once more a happy farewell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| WARWICK | Farewell, sweet lords—let’s meet at Coventry. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally! |
| *(didascalia)* | 4.10 Erenter [[King]] Henry and the [[Duke of Exeter]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Here at the palace will I rest a while.<br>Cousin of Exeter, what thinks your lordship?<br>Methinks the power that Edward hath in field<br>Should not be able to encounter mine. |
| EXETER | The [[Doubt\|doubt]] is that he will seduce the rest. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | That’s not my [[Fear\|fear]]. My meed hath got me fame.<br>I have not stopped mine ears to their demands,<br>Nor posted off their suits with slow delays.<br>My pity hath been balm to heal their wounds,<br>My mildness hath allayed their swelling griefs,<br>My [[Mercy\|mercy]] dried their [[Water\|water]]-flowing [[Tears\|tears]].<br>I have not been desirous of their wealth,<br>Nor much oppressed them with great subsidies,<br>Nor forward of [[Revenge\|revenge]], though they much erred.<br>Then why should they [[Love\|love]] Edward more than me?<br>No, Exeter, these graces challenge grace;<br>And when the [[Lion\|lion]] fawns upon the [[Lamb\|lamb]],<br>The [[Lamb\|lamb]] will never cease to follow him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Shout within ‘A Lancaster’, ‘A York’ |
| EXETER | Hark, hark, my ford—what shouts are these? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Edward and [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]], with soldiers |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Seize on the shame-faced Henry—bear him hence,<br>And once again proclaim us [[King]] of England.<br>You are the fount that makes small brooks to flow.<br>Now stops thy [[spring]]—my sea shall suck them dry,<br>And swell so much the higher by their ebb.<br>Hence with him to the Tower—tet him not speak. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt some with [[King]] Henry and Exeter |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | And lords, towards Coventry bend we our course,<br>Where peremptory Warwick now remains.<br>The sun shines hot, and, if we use delay,<br>Cold biting [[Winter\|winter]] mars our hoped-for hay. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Away betimes, before his forces join,<br>And take the great-grown traitor unawares.<br>Brave warriors, march amain towards Coventry. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]], the Mayar of Coventry, two Messengers, and others upon the walls |
| WARWICK | Where is the post that came from valiant [[Oxford]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | The First Messenger steps forward |
| WARWICK | How far hence is thy lord, mine honest fellow? |
| FIRST MESSENGER | By this at Dunsmore, marching hitherward. |
| WARWICK | How far off is our brother Montague?<br>Where is the post that came from Montague? |
| *(didascalia)* | the Second Messenger steps forward |
| SECOND MESSENGER | By this at Da’ntry, with a puissant troop. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Somerville to them, abovel |
| WARWICK | Say, Somerville-what says my loving son?<br>And, by thy guess, how nigh is [[Clarence]] now? |
| SOMERVILLE | At Southam I did leave him with his forces,<br>And do expect him here some two hours hence.<br>A march afar off |
| WARWICK | Then [[Clarence]] is at hand-I hear his drum. |
| SOMERVILLE | It is not his, my lord. Here Southam lies.<br>The drum your [[Honour\|honour]] hears marcheth from Warwick. |
| WARWICK | Who should that be? Belike, unlooked-for friends. |
| SOMERVILLE | They are at hand, and you shall quickly know. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter below [[King]] Edward and [[Richard]] [[Duke of Gloucester]], with soldiers |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Go, trumpet, to the walls, and sound a parley. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound a parley |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | See how the surly Warwick mans the wall. |
| WARWICK | O, unbid spite—is sportful Edward come?<br>Where slept our scouts, or how are they seduced,<br>That we could hear no news of his repair? |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, Warwick, wilt thou ope the [[City\|city]] gates,<br>Speak gentle words, and humbly bend thy knee,<br>Call Edward [[King\|king]], and at his hands beg [[Mercy\|mercy]]?<br>And he shall pardon thee these outrages. |
| WARWICK | Nay, rather, wilt thou draw thy forces hence,<br>Confess who set thee up and plucked thee down,<br>Call Warwick patron, and be penitent?<br>And thou shalt still remain the [[Duke of York]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I thought at least he would have said ‘the [[King]]’.<br>Or did he make the jest against his will? |
| WARWICK | Is not a dukedom, sir, a goodly gift? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Ay, by my [[Faith\|faith]], for a poor earl to give.<br>I’ll do thee service for so good a gift. |
| WARWICK | ’Twas I that gave the kingdom to thy brother. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Why then, ’tis mine, if but by Warwick’s gift. |
| WARWICK | Thou art no Atlas for so great a weight;<br>And, weakling, Warwick takes his gift again;<br>And Henry is my [[King\|king]], Warwick his subject. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | But Warwick’s [[King\|king]] is Edward’s prisoner,<br>And, gallant Warwick, do but answer this:<br>What is the [[Body\|body]] when the head is off? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Alas, that Warwick had no more forecast,<br>But whiles he thought to steal the single ten,<br>The [[King\|king]] was slyly fingered from the deck. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Warwick You left poor Henry at the Bishop’s palace, |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And ten to one you’ll meet him in the Tower. |
| *(didascalia)* | ’Tis even so—to Warwick yet you are Warwick still. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Come, Warwick, take the [[Time\|time]]—kneel down, kneel<br>down.<br>Nay, when? Strike now, or else the iron cools. |
| WARWICK | I had rather chop this hand off at a blow,<br>And with the other fling it at thy face,<br>Than bear so low a sail to strike to thee. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Sail how thou canst, have [[Wind\|wind]] and tide thy friend,<br>This hand, fast wound about thy coal-black hair,<br>Shall, whiles thy head is warm and new cut off,<br>Write in the [[Dust\|dust]] this sentence with thy [[Blood\|blood]]:<br>‘[[Wind]]-changing Warwick now can change no more’. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earl of [[Oxford]], with a drummer and soldiers bearing colours |
| WARWICK | O cheerful colours! See where [[Oxford]] comes. |
| [[Oxford\|OXFORD]] | [[Oxford]], [[Oxford]], for Lancaster! |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Oxford]] and his men pass over the stage and exeunt into the [[City\|city]] |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to [[King]] Edward)<br>The gates are open—let us enter too. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | So other foes may set upon our backs?<br>Stand we in good array, for they no [[Doubt\|doubt]]<br>Will issue out again and bid us battle.<br>If not, the [[City\|city]] being but of small defence,<br>We’ll quickly rouse the traitors in the same. |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICK to [[Oxford]], within |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | O, welcome, [[Oxford]]—for we want thy help. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Marquis of Montague with a drummer and soldiers bearing colours |
| MONTAGUE | Montague, Montague, for Lancaster! |
| *(didascalia)* | Montague and his men pass over the stage and exeunt into the [[City\|city]] |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Thou and thy brother both shall bye this treason<br>Even with the dearest [[Blood\|blood]] your bodies bear. |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | The harder matched, the greater victory.<br>My mind presageth happy gain and conquest. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke of Somerset with a drummer and soldiers bearing colours |
| SOMERSET | Somerset, Somerset, for Lancaster! |
| *(didascalia)* | Somerset and his men pass over the stage and exeunt into the [[City\|city]] |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Two of thy name, both dukes of Somerset,<br>Have sold their lives unto the [[house]] of York—<br>And thou shalt be the third, an this [[Sword\|sword]] hold. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter George Duke of [[Clarence]] with a drummer and soldiers bearing colours |
| WARWICK | And lo, where George of [[Clarence]] sweeps along,<br>Of force enough to bid his brother battle;<br>With whom an upright zeal to right prevails<br>More than the [[Nature\|nature]] of a brother’s [[Love\|love]]. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | [[Clarence]], [[Clarence]], for Lancaster! |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | Et tu, Brute—wilt thou stab Caesar too?<br>(To a trumpeter) A parley, sirra, to George of [[Clarence]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound a parley. [[Richard]] of [[Gloucester]] and George of |
| [[King\|KING]] EDWARD | [[Clarence]] whisper together |
| WARWICK | Come, [[Clarence]], come—thou wilt if Warwick call. |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Father of Warwick, know you what this means? |
| *(didascalia)* | He takes his red [[Rose\|rose]] out of his hat and throws it at Warwick |
| GEORGE OF [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Look—here I throw my infamy at thee!<br>I will not ruinate my father’s [[house]],<br>Who gave his [[Blood\|blood]] to lime the stones together,<br>And set up Lancaster. Why, trowest thou, Warwick,<br>That [[Clarence]] is so harsh, so blunt, unnatural,<br>To bend the fatal instruments of war<br>Against his brother and his lawful [[King\|king]]?<br>Perhaps thou wilt object my holy oath.<br>To keep that oath were more impiety<br>Than Jephthah, when he sacrificed his daughter.<br>I am so sorry for my trespass made<br>That, to deserve [[Well\|well]] at my brothers’ hands,<br>I here proclaim myself thy mortal foe,<br>With resolution, wheresoe’er I meet thee—<br>As I will meet thee, if thou stir abroad—<br>To plague thee for thy foul misleading me.<br>And so, proud-hearted Warwick, I defy thee,<br>And to my brothers turn my blushing cheeks.<br>(To [[King]] Edward)<br>Pardon me, Edward—I will make amends.<br>(To [[Richard]])<br>And, [[Richard]], do not frown upon my faults,<br>For I will henceforth be no more unconstant. |
